<div id="anp_a" class="d-panel p" style="display: none"><div class="notes p"><section id="historyarticle-meta1" class="history"><p>Received 2023 Aug 10; Accepted 2024 Sep 26; Collection date 2023 Dec.</p></section></div></div>
<div id="clp_a" class="d-panel p" style="display: none">
<div>© 2024 by the Society for Experimental Biology and Medicine</div>
<div class="p"><a href="/about/copyright/" class="usa-link">PMC Copyright notice</a></div>
</div>
</div>
<div>PMCID: PMC10854475  PMID: <a href="https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/38281087/" class="usa-link">38281087</a>
</div>
</div></section></section><section aria-label="Article content"><section class="body main-article-body"><section class="abstract" id="abstract1"><h2>Abstract</h2>
<p>Data imbalance is a challenging problem in classification tasks, and when combined with class overlapping, it further deteriorates classification performance. However, existing studies have rarely addressed both issues simultaneously. In this article, we propose a novel quantum-based oversampling method (QOSM) to effectively tackle data imbalance and class overlapping, thereby improving classification performance. QOSM utilizes the quantum potential theory to calculate the potential energy of each sample and selects the sample with the lowest potential as the center of each cover generated by a constructive covering algorithm. This approach optimizes cover center selection and better captures the distribution of the original samples, particularly in the overlapping regions. In addition, oversampling is performed on the samples of the minority class covers to mitigate the imbalance ratio (IR). We evaluated QOSM using three traditional classifiers (support vector machines [SVM], k-nearest neighbor [KNN], and naive Bayes [NB] classifier) on 10 publicly available KEEL data sets characterized by high IRs and varying degrees of overlap. Experimental results demonstrate that QOSM significantly improves classification accuracy compared to approaches that do not address class imbalance and overlapping. Moreover, QOSM consistently outperforms existing oversampling methods tested. With its compatibility with different classifiers, QOSM exhibits promising potential to improve the classification performance of highly imbalanced and overlapped data.</p>
<section id="kwd-group1" class="kwd-group"><p><strong>Keywords:</strong> Classification, class imbalance, class overlapping, quantum potential energy, oversampling</p></section></section><section id="section1-15353702231220665"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">Impact statement</h2>
<p>Accurate classification and analysis of imbalanced and overlapped data, which are prevalent in the fields of biology and medical sciences, are crucial for understanding complex biological systems, including disease diagnosis, drug discovery, and personalized medicine. This research introduces quantum-based oversampling method (QOSM), a novel oversampling method based on quantum potential theory, addressing challenges in highly imbalanced and overlapped data sets. QOSM improves sample distribution representation, especially in overlapped regions, by selecting cover centers using potential energy. Compared to existing methods like SMOTE (synthetic minority oversampling technique), QOSM considers individual sample and cover relationships, effectively reducing mislabeled synthetic samples. Extensive evaluations on 10 data sets with high imbalance ratios and overlap demonstrate that QOSM significantly improves classification accuracy, outperforming other methods across multiple classifiers, such as support vector machines (SVM), k-nearest neighbor (KNN), and naive Bayes (NB) classifier. Therefore, QOSM presents a novel and effective approach to address imbalance and overlap in classification, with great potential for application in various biological and medical data sets.</p></section><section id="section2-15353702231220665"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">Introduction</h2>
<p>Improving the performance of classification on imbalanced data, where the number of samples of the majority class greatly exceeds that of the minority class, has been a significant challenge in the field of machine learning. Imbalanced classification is prevalent in various fields, such as biological science and medical diagnosis. For instance, it is observed in imbalanced class learning in epigenetics,<sup>
<a href="#bibr1-15353702231220665" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bibr1-15353702231220665">1</a>
</sup> readmission risk prediction in clinical and medical fields to support diagnostic decision-making,<sup>
<a href="#bibr2-15353702231220665" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bibr2-15353702231220665">2</a>
</sup> and chemical classification and toxicity analysis.<sup>
<a href="#bibr3-15353702231220665" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bibr3-15353702231220665">3</a>
</sup> When traditional classifiers are applied to these imbalanced data sets, they often exhibit deficiencies.<sup>
<a href="#bibr4-15353702231220665" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bibr4-15353702231220665">4</a>
</sup> Consequently, numerous researchers have been motivated to develop different approaches to address the imbalanced data set problem and improve classification accuracy.<sup>
<a href="#bibr5-15353702231220665" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bibr5-15353702231220665">5</a>
</sup></p>
<p>Research in this area can be categorized into algorithm- and data-level approaches. Algorithm-level approaches involve the development of new algorithms or modifications to existing ones,<sup>
<a href="#bibr6-15353702231220665" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bibr6-15353702231220665">6</a>
</sup> tailored to the characteristics of imbalanced data, with the aim of achieving higher classification accuracy. On the other hand, data-level approaches primarily focus on reducing the imbalance ratio (IR),<sup>
<a href="#bibr7-15353702231220665" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bibr7-15353702231220665">7</a>
</sup> which is defined as the ratio of the number of the majority class samples to the number of minority class samples. This reduction is typically accomplished through resampling techniques,<sup>
<a href="#bibr8-15353702231220665" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bibr8-15353702231220665">8</a>
</sup> which aim to balance the distribution of samples across different classes. In data-level approaches, classification algorithms and data remain independent of each other, allowing existing classifiers to be applied to classification tasks without significant modification. In this article, our focus is on a data-level approach for addressing the challenges posed by imbalance data. We employ existing classifiers for classification purposes while leveraging techniques to handle the imbalance in the data set.</p>
<p>Classification of imbalanced data has seen widespread use of downsampling and oversampling techniques. Downsampling involves randomly eliminating samples from the majority class, which may result in the loss of valuable information contained within these removed samples. Conversely, in oversampling techniques, samples from the minority class are randomly selected and added repeatedly to the training data, potentially leading to overfitting. Recognizing the limitations of downsampling and oversampling, researchers have proposed methods to generate synthetic data instances using samples from the minority class for imbalance handling. Notable among those methods is SMOTE (synthetic minority oversampling technique)<sup>
<a href="#bibr9-15353702231220665" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bibr9-15353702231220665">9</a>
</sup> and its variants, which are introduced in the next section of this article. Many publications have demonstrated the effectiveness of these synthetic data generation methods in addressing class imbalances. These methods have been shown to frequently outperform traditional downsampling and oversampling techniques.<sup>
<a href="#bibr5-15353702231220665" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bibr5-15353702231220665">5</a>
</sup> Therefore, our focus in this article is on oversampling techniques that generate synthetic data instances utilizing samples from the minority class.</p>
<p>Many real-world data sets exhibit not only class imbalance but also class overlap. Class overlap occurs when the samples from two classes share similar features but have different class labels, making it difficult to separate them. When a data set is both highly imbalanced and overlapped, the combined effect of these two factors becomes more intricate.<sup><a href="#bibr10-15353702231220665" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bibr10-15353702231220665">10</a>,<a href="#bibr11-15353702231220665" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bibr11-15353702231220665">11</a></sup> In such cases, defining the decision boundary for classification becomes difficult, and traditional classifiers are prone to misclassifying the minority class samples in the overlapping area as belonging to the majority class. As a consequence, the classification boundary tends to shift toward the minority class area, as depicted in <a href="#fig1-15353702231220665" class="usa-link">Figure 1</a>.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="fig1-15353702231220665"><h3 class="obj_head">Figure 1.</h3>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><img class="graphic" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/a261/10854475/496f431c5d3a/10.1177_15353702231220665-fig1.jpg" loading="lazy" height="771" width="635" alt="Figure 1."></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/fig1-15353702231220665/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>(a) The classification boundary of the original data set. (b) The classification boundary after changing the class overlap distribution.</p></figcaption></figure><p>Most existing approaches have been designed to address either class imbalance or class overlap independently. However, in numerous real-world applications, these problems coexist, and data sets often exhibit higher imbalance and overlap ratios.<sup><a href="#bibr12-15353702231220665" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bibr12-15353702231220665">12</a>,<a href="#bibr13-15353702231220665" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bibr13-15353702231220665">13</a></sup> A comprehensive review and analysis of previous studies on imbalance and class overlap can be found in Vuttipittayamongkol <em>et al</em>.,<sup>
<a href="#bibr11-15353702231220665" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bibr11-15353702231220665">11</a>
</sup> and Santos <em>et al</em>.<sup>
<a href="#bibr14-15353702231220665" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bibr14-15353702231220665">14</a>
</sup> The analysis concluded that class overlap can have varying degrees of negative impact on classification results, whereas imbalance does not always have such an effect. However, when imbalance and class overlap are present simultaneously, the influence of class overlap becomes more pronounced. Consequently, studying data sets that are highly imbalanced and highly overlapped holds significance importance.</p>
<p>In this article, we propose a novel quantum-based oversampling method (QOSM) that addresses the challenges of data imbalance and overlapping simultaneously, with the goal of improving classification performance. QOSM utilizes cover structures to describe the data distribution and determine the decision boundary of the classification. Specifically, it focuses on changing the minority class distribution in the overlapping area. Each cover is represented by its center and radius.</p>
<p>Existing cover construction algorithms (CCAs)<sup>
<a href="#bibr15-15353702231220665" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bibr15-15353702231220665">15</a>
</sup> often rely on random selection of cover centers, which may not be suitable, especially in the overlapping area. To tackle this limitation, we introduce the principles of quantum theory to describe the data distribution and determine cover centers. In quantum theory, the distribution states of microscopic particles within an energy field are determined by their energy levels. Particles with low potential energy exhibit increased stability and tend to cluster together. In the context of data analysis, we apply the concept of “quantum potential” to characterize data samples. This potential energy field is computed based on the distances or similarities between data samples. Quantum potential theory offers a distinctive approach to address clustering or covering challenges because it takes into account the intricate interactions and relationships among data points, inspired by principles from quantum mechanics. Motivated by these observations and the pioneering work of Horn,<sup>
<a href="#bibr16-15353702231220665" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bibr16-15353702231220665">16</a>
</sup> we leverage quantum potential theory to optimize the process of determining cover centers in the CCA. In this approach, each data sample is analogously treated as a particle, and the particular samples with zero or minimum potential energy are considered central and are surrounded by more neighboring samples, making them suitable candidates for cover centers or cluster representatives.</p>
<p>QOSM aims to tackle the main cause of boundary migration in the classification of highly imbalanced and overlapped data. By increasing the number of minority class samples in the overlapping region, it can alter the minority class distribution, and partially shift the classification boundary toward the majority class, as illustrated in <a href="#fig1-15353702231220665" class="usa-link">Figure 1(b)</a>. This method effectively addresses class overlapping while simultaneously reducing the IR. We expect that the oversampling method with quantum-based cover construction will improve classification accuracy for highly imbalanced and overlapped data.</p>
<p>The main contributions of this article are as follows:</p>
<ol id="list1-15353702231220665" class="list" style="list-style-type:decimal">
<li><p>We propose a novel quantum-based oversampling method for the classification of highly imbalanced and overlapped data.</p></li>
<li><p>The CCA is improved by incorporating the quantum potential energy theory, which better captures the spatial distribution of data in the overlapped area.</p></li>
<li><p>We demonstrate the superiority of QOSM over the state-of-the-art sampling methods through experiments on several extremely imbalanced and overlapped real-world data sets.</p></li>
</ol></section><section id="section3-15353702231220665"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">Related work</h2>
<p>Several factors significantly impact the performance of classification on the imbalanced and overlapped data, including IR, the degree of overlap, the quantity of training samples, the ratio of noise samples, and the severity of intraclass sub-aggregation. Data-level sampling methods aim to mitigate the influence of these factors and improve classification performance. SMOTE<sup>
<a href="#bibr9-15353702231220665" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bibr9-15353702231220665">9</a>
</sup> is a conventional oversampling method used to reduce the IR by synthesizing new minority class instances between existing minority class samples. However, SMOTE has limitations, such as potentially generating mislabeled synthetic samples and increasing the risk of class overlap, especially in data sets containing noise. Consequently, learning from data sets processed by SMOTE often leads to a higher false prediction rate.<sup>
<a href="#bibr17-15353702231220665" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bibr17-15353702231220665">17</a>
</sup> To address these issues, several variants of SMOTE have been developed. Han proposed the borderline-SMOTE method,<sup>
<a href="#bibr18-15353702231220665" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bibr18-15353702231220665">18</a>
</sup> which focuses on oversampling borderline samples under the assumption that samples located in the boundary areas between the two classes play a more crucial role in classification. He <em>et al</em>.<sup>
<a href="#bibr19-15353702231220665" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bibr19-15353702231220665">19</a>
</sup> introduced the ADASYN (Adaptive Synthetic) method, which adaptively synthesizes new instances by considering the density distribution information of minority class samples. Clustering-based methods, such as Cluster_SMOTE<sup>
<a href="#bibr20-15353702231220665" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bibr20-15353702231220665">20</a>
</sup> and DBSMOTE<sup>
<a href="#bibr21-15353702231220665" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bibr21-15353702231220665">21</a>
</sup> take into consideration dense or sparse subclusters of minority and majority instances.</p>
<p>Class imbalance generally has a detrimental effect on classification performance. Existing methods for addressing this problem can be categorized as follows. One approach involves using a kernel function to map the original data to a high dimensional feature space, aiming to increase the separability between classes.<sup>
<a href="#bibr22-15353702231220665" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bibr22-15353702231220665">22</a>
</sup> Another category of methods focuses on addressing class overlap through data cleaning techniques, such as SMOTE + ENN (Edited Nearest Neighbors) and SMOTE + TomekLinks.<sup>
<a href="#bibr23-15353702231220665" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bibr23-15353702231220665">23</a>
</sup> In the SMOTE–ENN algorithm, synthetic instances are generated using SMOTE based on the minority samples. The class label of each synthetic instance is compared with the majority vote of its k-nearest neighbors (KNNs). Only the instances with consistent class labels are retained. Our previous work also demonstrated that the removal of mislabeled instances in SMOTE–ENN can improve the classification accuracy.<sup>
<a href="#bibr3-15353702231220665" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bibr3-15353702231220665">3</a>
</sup> Vuttipittayamongkol proposed a neighborhood-based undersampling framework for handling class imbalance in binary data sets, which involves removing potentially overlapping data points and enhancing the visibility of minority class instances.<sup>
<a href="#bibr12-15353702231220665" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bibr12-15353702231220665">12</a>
</sup> Another interesting approach involves detecting overlapping regions and employing different strategies for classification in these regions.<sup>
<a href="#bibr24-15353702231220665" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bibr24-15353702231220665">24</a>
</sup> However, these methods may lead to the loss of important information in datasets due to undersampling or fail to fully leverage the distribution and data structure within the overlapping region.</p>
<p>In recent years, there has been significant research activity in developing and improving quantum-based methods for clustering and classification. Quantum clustering methods were initially introduced by Horn and Gottlieb<sup>
<a href="#bibr16-15353702231220665" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bibr16-15353702231220665">16</a>
</sup> who drew inspiration from the observation that data points in a feature space can be associated with a Schrödinger equation, where the potential is determined by the data. Weinstein and Horn<sup>
<a href="#bibr25-15353702231220665" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bibr25-15353702231220665">25</a>
</sup> further advanced this approach by developing a dynamic quantum clustering method using a time-dependent Schrödinger equation. Scott <em>et al</em>.<sup>
<a href="#bibr26-15353702231220665" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bibr26-15353702231220665">26</a>
</sup> demonstrated that quantum mechanics serves as the foundation for several useful data clustering methods. Maignan and Scott<sup>
<a href="#bibr27-15353702231220665" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bibr27-15353702231220665">27</a>
</sup> explored the mathematical task of finding all the roots of the exponential polynomial corresponding to the minima of a two-dimensional quantum potential and established that, if the points are contained within a square region of a specific size, there exists only one minimum. Decheng <em>et al</em>.<sup>
<a href="#bibr28-15353702231220665" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bibr28-15353702231220665">28</a>
</sup> introduced a distance measure that enhances quantum clustering analysis by projecting non-spherical overlapping data in the Euclidean space onto a weighted Euclidean space, effectively creating non-overlapping data. Tian <em>et al</em>.<sup>
<a href="#bibr29-15353702231220665" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bibr29-15353702231220665">29</a>
</sup> proposed a quantum clustering ensemble (QCE) technique derived from quantum mechanics, offering another approach to clustering tasks. Quantum clustering has also been successfully applied in data mining applications, such as outlier detection and text analysis.<sup>
<a href="#bibr30-15353702231220665" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bibr30-15353702231220665">30</a>
</sup> Li and Kais<sup>
<a href="#bibr31-15353702231220665" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bibr31-15353702231220665">31</a>
</sup> presented a quantum algorithm for data classification based on the nearest neighbor learning algorithm. These methods have demonstrated the potential of quantum approaches in clustering and classification tasks. However, these existing methods directly applied the quantum theory for clustering and classification, without specifically addressing the challenges posed by the imbalance and overlapping data sets. Our QOSM takes a different approach by utilizing quantum theory to construct optimal covers from overlapped data. These covers are then employed to oversample the imbalanced data sets, effectively addressing both imbalance and overlap. Notably, our novel oversampling method is independent of classifiers, allowing for the applications of various advanced classification methods in subsequent classification tasks.</p></section><section id="section4-15353702231220665"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">Materials and methods</h2>
<p>We introduce a novel quantum-based oversampling method designed specifically for highly imbalanced and overlapped data in classification tasks. Our approach utilizes CCA,<sup><a href="#bibr15-15353702231220665" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bibr15-15353702231220665">15</a>,<a href="#bibr32-15353702231220665" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bibr32-15353702231220665">32</a></sup> to create a set of disjoint covers that represent the data distribution. Each cover consists of a center and radius, and samples within a cover share the same class label. While CCA provides a foundation for exploring spatial characteristics, the selection of cover center is traditionally random, which may lead to suboptimal classification performance, especially for highly imbalanced and overlapped data. To address this, we improve the cover center selection by ranking the quantum potential energy of each sample, resulting in more representative covers. Subsequently, we utilize these optimized covers to investigate the sample distribution, with a particular focus on identifying minority class samples in the overlapping area.</p>
<p>Our approach considers both individual sample relationship and cover relationships to capture the data set’s sample distribution. Initially, we apply the improved cover construction method to the original data set, taking into account the distribution characteristics of each individual sample. In our approach, we partition the minority class sample space into a set of covers, while also dividing the majority class samples into their respective covers. Our subsequent step involves identifying the minority class covers within the overlapping area. Typically, samples located in the regions of class overlap and the boundary region between classes are more susceptible to misclassification. The address this, we employ the KNN method to explore the distribution relationship between covers and extract the minority class covers situated in the overlapping area. Once the relevant covers are identified, we perform oversampling on the samples belonging to the minority class covers, generating synthetic instances to address the data imbalance. For a more detailed understanding of our approach, in the following subsections, we provide an overview of the CCA that outlines the process of cover construction, a discussion of quantum potential theory that explains its role in optimizing cover selection, and the technical details of our proposed QOSM that encompasses the steps involved in identifying minority class covers in the overlapping area and conducting oversampling to generate synthetic instances.</p>
<section id="section5-15353702231220665"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Cover construction algorithms</h3>
<p>CCAs play an important role in our proposed method. CCA is a domain construction algorithm that constructs covers based on the distance between samples.<sup>
<a href="#bibr15-15353702231220665" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bibr15-15353702231220665">15</a>
</sup> Each cover consists of multiple samples and is characterized by a cover center and cover radius. These covers exhibit the following characteristics: (1) samples within the same cover share the same class label; (2) if samples with the same label exhibit significant differences, they are assigned to separate covers; and (3) samples belonging to different categories cannot be projected onto the same cover.</p>
<p>To provide an overview of the CCA, let us consider an input data set <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="inline-formula1-15353702231220665">

</span>, where <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="inline-formula2-15353702231220665">
<math id="math2-15353702231220665" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mi>p</mi></mrow></math>
</span> represents the number of samples in the data set <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="inline-formula3-15353702231220665">

</span> and <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="inline-formula5-15353702231220665">

</span> sample with its corresponding label, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="inline-formula7-15353702231220665">
<math id="math7-15353702231220665" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mi>n</mi></mrow></math>

</span> feature of the sample <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="inline-formula11-15353702231220665">
<math id="math11-15353702231220665" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><msub><mi>x</mi><mi>i</mi></msub></mrow></math>
</span>. The output of the CCA is a set of covers C, defined as <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="inline-formula12-15353702231220665">

</span> cover of class <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="inline-formula15-15353702231220665">
<math id="math15-15353702231220665" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mi>i</mi></mrow></math>
</span>. <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="inline-formula16-15353702231220665">
<math id="math16-15353702231220665" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><msub><mi>C</mi><mi>i</mi></msub><mo>=</mo><mo>∪</mo><msubsup><mi>C</mi><mi>i</mi><mi>j</mi></msubsup></mrow></math>
</span> encompasses all the covers of class <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="inline-formula17-15353702231220665">
<math id="math17-15353702231220665" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mi>i</mi></mrow></math>
</span>. The main steps of CCA are similar to that in, Zhang and Zhang<sup>
<a href="#bibr15-15353702231220665" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bibr15-15353702231220665">15</a>
</sup>, Guliyev and Ismailov<sup>
<a href="#bibr32-15353702231220665" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bibr32-15353702231220665">32</a>
</sup> briefly described as follows:</p>
<ul id="list2-15353702231220665" class="list" style="list-style-type:none"><li><p>Step 1: Data normalization</p></li></ul>

</tr></table>
<p>where <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="inline-formula23-15353702231220665">
<math id="math25-15353702231220665" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mi>R</mi></mrow></math>
</span> denotes the radius of the sphere <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="inline-formula24-15353702231220665">
<math id="math26-15353702231220665" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><msup><mi>S</mi><mi>n</mi></msup></mrow></math>
</span>, which is set to the maximum value of <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="inline-formula25-15353702231220665">
<math id="math27-15353702231220665" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mo>|</mo><mrow><msub><mi>x</mi><mi>i</mi></msub></mrow><mo>|</mo></mrow></mrow></math>
</span>. <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="inline-formula26-15353702231220665">
<math id="math28-15353702231220665" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><msub><mi>x</mi><mi>i</mi></msub><mo>=</mo><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msubsup><mi>x</mi><mi>i</mi><mn>1</mn></msubsup><mo>,</mo><msubsup><mi>x</mi><mi>i</mi><mn>2</mn></msubsup><mo>,</mo><mo>…</mo><mo>,</mo><msubsup><mi>x</mi><mi>i</mi><mi>n</mi></msubsup><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></math>
</span> represents the <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="inline-formula27-15353702231220665">
<math id="math29-15353702231220665" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mi>i</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">th</mi></mrow></math>
</span> sample of the data set <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="inline-formula28-15353702231220665">
<math id="math30-15353702231220665" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mi>X</mi></mrow></math>
</span> and <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="inline-formula29-15353702231220665">
<math id="math31-15353702231220665" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mo>|</mo><mrow><msub><mi>x</mi><mi>i</mi></msub></mrow><mo>|</mo></mrow><mo>=</mo><msqrt><mrow><msup><mrow><mrow><mo>(</mo><mrow><msubsup><mi>x</mi><mi>i</mi><mn>1</mn></msubsup></mrow><mo>)</mo></mrow></mrow><mn>2</mn></msup><mo>+</mo><msup><mrow><mrow><mo>(</mo><mrow><msubsup><mi>x</mi><mi>i</mi><mn>2</mn></msubsup></mrow><mo>)</mo></mrow></mrow><mn>2</mn></msup><mo>+</mo><mo>…</mo><mo>+</mo><msup><mrow><mrow><mo>(</mo><mrow><msubsup><mi>x</mi><mi>i</mi><mi>n</mi></msubsup></mrow><mo>)</mo></mrow></mrow><mn>2</mn></msup></mrow></msqrt></mrow></math>
</span>.</p>
<ul id="list4-15353702231220665" class="list" style="list-style-type:none"><li><p>Step 3: Cover construction</p></li></ul>
<p>Each cover is determined by a cover center <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="inline-formula30-15353702231220665">
<math id="math32-15353702231220665" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><msub><mi>x</mi><mi>k</mi></msub></mrow></math>
</span> and radius <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="inline-formula31-15353702231220665">
<math id="math33-15353702231220665" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mi>r</mi></mrow></math>
</span>. The cover center is randomly selected, and the cover radius is determined as the average of <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="inline-formula32-15353702231220665">
<math id="math34-15353702231220665" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><msub><mi>d</mi><mrow><mi>i</mi><mi>n</mi><mi>t</mi><mi>e</mi><mi>r</mi></mrow></msub></mrow></math>
</span> and <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="inline-formula33-15353702231220665">
<math id="math35-15353702231220665" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><msub><mi>d</mi><mrow><mi>i</mi><mi>n</mi><mi>n</mi><mi>e</mi><mi>r</mi></mrow></msub></mrow></math>
</span>, satisfying <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="inline-formula34-15353702231220665">
<math id="math36-15353702231220665" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><msub><mi>d</mi><mrow><mi>i</mi><mi>n</mi><mi>n</mi><mi>e</mi><mi>r</mi></mrow></msub><mo>&lt;</mo><msub><mi>d</mi><mrow><mi>i</mi><mi>n</mi><mi>t</mi><mi>e</mi><mi>r</mi></mrow></msub></mrow></math>
</span>, as illustrated in <a href="#fig2-15353702231220665" class="usa-link">Figure 2</a>. Here, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="inline-formula35-15353702231220665">
<math id="math37-15353702231220665" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><msub><mi>d</mi><mrow><mi>i</mi><mi>n</mi><mi>t</mi><mi>e</mi><mi>r</mi></mrow></msub></mrow></math>
</span> represents the minimum distance of samples with different class labels from <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="inline-formula36-15353702231220665">
<math id="math38-15353702231220665" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><msub><mi>x</mi><mi>k</mi></msub></mrow></math>
</span>, while <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="inline-formula37-15353702231220665">
<math id="math39-15353702231220665" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><msub><mi>d</mi><mrow><mi>i</mi><mi>n</mi><mi>n</mi><mi>e</mi><mi>r</mi></mrow></msub></mrow></math>
</span> denotes the maximum distance between samples with the same class label from the cover center <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="inline-formula38-15353702231220665">
<math id="math40-15353702231220665" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><msub><mi>x</mi><mi>k</mi></msub></mrow></math>
</span>. The distance between samples is measured by the vector inner product, as shown in <a href="#disp-formula3-15353702231220665" class="usa-link">equation (3)</a>. The cover radius <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="inline-formula39-15353702231220665">
<math id="math41-15353702231220665" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mi>r</mi></mrow></math>
</span> can be obtained by <a href="#disp-formula4-15353702231220665" class="usa-link">equation (4)</a> through <a href="#disp-formula6-15353702231220665" class="usa-link">equation (6)</a>.</p>

</tr></table>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="disp-formula4-15353702231220665"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="math43-15353702231220665" display="block" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><msub><mi>d</mi><mrow><mi>int</mi><mi>e</mi><mi>r</mi></mrow></msub><mo>=</mo><munder><mrow><mi>m</mi><mi>a</mi><mi>x</mi></mrow><mrow><msub><mi>y</mi><mi>i</mi></msub><mo>≠</mo><msub><mi>y</mi><mi>k</mi></msub></mrow></munder><mo>{</mo><mo>〈</mo><msub><mi>x</mi><mi>k</mi></msub><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>x</mi><mi>i</mi></msub><mo>〉</mo><mo>}</mo><mo>,</mo><mi>i</mi><mo>∈</mo><mo>{</mo><mn>1</mn><mo>,</mo><mn>2</mn><mo>,</mo><mo>…</mo><mo>,</mo><mi>p</mi><mo>}</mo></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">(4)</td>
</tr></table>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="disp-formula5-15353702231220665"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="math44-15353702231220665" display="block" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><msub><mi>d</mi><mrow><mi>i</mi><mi>n</mi><mi>n</mi><mi>e</mi><mi>r</mi></mrow></msub><mo>=</mo><munder><mrow><mi>m</mi><mi>i</mi><mi>n</mi></mrow><mrow><msub><mi>y</mi><mi>i</mi></msub><mo>=</mo><msub><mi>y</mi><mi>k</mi></msub></mrow></munder><mo>{</mo><mo>〈</mo><msub><mi>x</mi><mi>k</mi></msub><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>x</mi><mi>i</mi></msub><mo>〉</mo><mrow><mo>|</mo><mrow><mo>〈</mo><msub><mi>x</mi><mi>k</mi></msub><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>x</mi><mi>i</mi></msub><mo>〉</mo><mo>&gt;</mo><msub><mi>d</mi><mrow><mi>int</mi><mi>e</mi><mi>r</mi></mrow></msub></mrow></mrow><mo>}</mo><mo>,</mo><mi>i</mi><mo>∈</mo><mo>{</mo><mn>1</mn><mo>,</mo><mn>2</mn><mo>,</mo><mo>…</mo><mo>,</mo><mi>p</mi><mo>}</mo></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">(5)</td>
</tr></table>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="disp-formula6-15353702231220665"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="math45-15353702231220665" display="block" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mi>r</mi><mo>=</mo><mfrac><mrow><msub><mi>d</mi><mrow><mi>int</mi><mi>e</mi><mi>r</mi></mrow></msub><mo>+</mo><msub><mi>d</mi><mrow><mi>i</mi><mi>n</mi><mi>n</mi><mi>e</mi><mi>r</mi></mrow></msub></mrow><mn>2</mn></mfrac></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">(6)</td>
</tr></table>
<p>In <a href="#disp-formula4-15353702231220665" class="usa-link">equation (4)</a>, the maximum inner product, which represents the minimum distance from samples between different classes. Here, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="inline-formula43-15353702231220665">
<math id="math49-15353702231220665" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><msub><mi>y</mi><mi>k</mi></msub></mrow></math>
</span> and <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="inline-formula44-15353702231220665">
<math id="math50-15353702231220665" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><msub><mi>y</mi><mi>i</mi></msub></mrow></math>
</span> denote the categories of cover center <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="inline-formula45-15353702231220665">
<math id="math51-15353702231220665" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><msub><mi>x</mi><mi>k</mi></msub></mrow></math>
</span> and sample <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="inline-formula46-15353702231220665">
<math id="math52-15353702231220665" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><msub><mi>x</mi><mi>i</mi></msub></mrow></math>
</span>, respectively. When <em>y</em><sub>
<em>i</em>
</sub> ≠ <em>y</em><sub>
<em>k</em>
</sub>, it indicates that <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="inline-formula47-15353702231220665">
<math id="math53-15353702231220665" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><msub><mi>x</mi><mi>k</mi></msub></mrow></math>
</span> and <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="inline-formula48-15353702231220665">
<math id="math54-15353702231220665" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><msub><mi>x</mi><mi>i</mi></msub></mrow></math>
</span> belong to different categories. The value <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="inline-formula49-15353702231220665">
<math id="math55-15353702231220665" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mi>p</mi></mrow></math>
</span> represents the number of samples in data set <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="inline-formula50-15353702231220665">
<math id="math56-15353702231220665" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mi>X</mi></mrow></math>
</span>. Similarly, <a href="#disp-formula5-15353702231220665" class="usa-link">equation (5)</a> calculates the minimum inner product, representing the maximum distance between samples of the same class. In this case, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="inline-formula51-15353702231220665">
<math id="math57-15353702231220665" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><msub><mi>y</mi><mi>i</mi></msub><mo>=</mo><msub><mi>y</mi><mi>k</mi></msub></mrow></math>
</span> indicates that <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="inline-formula52-15353702231220665">
<math id="math58-15353702231220665" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><msub><mi>x</mi><mi>k</mi></msub></mrow></math>
</span> and <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="inline-formula53-15353702231220665">
<math id="math59-15353702231220665" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><msub><mi>x</mi><mi>i</mi></msub></mrow></math>
</span> are samples belonging to the same category.</p>
<ul id="list5-15353702231220665" class="list" style="list-style-type:none"><li><p>Step 4: By iterating Step 3, we continue constructing covers until all samples in the data space are covered.</p></li></ul></section><section id="section6-15353702231220665"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Quantum potential theory</h3>
<p>In our proposed method, we leverage the theory of quantum potential energy in the cover construction process to improve the selection of cover centers. This subsection introduces the relevant theory of quantum potential energy and describes how the potential energy of each sample is calculated.</p>
<p>In the field of quantum mechanics,<sup>
<a href="#bibr33-15353702231220665" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bibr33-15353702231220665">33</a>
</sup> a wave function represents the quantum state of a particle. Traditionally, the Schrödinger equation is solved to determine the particle distribution in various potential fields to obtain the corresponding wave function. The time-independent Schrödinger equation is given by:</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="disp-formula7-15353702231220665"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="math60-15353702231220665" display="block" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mi>H</mi><mi>ϕ</mi><mrow><mo>(</mo><mi>x</mi><mo>)</mo></mrow><mo>≡</mo><mrow><mo>(</mo><mrow><mo>−</mo><mfrac><mrow><msup><mi>δ</mi><mn>2</mn></msup></mrow><mn>2</mn></mfrac><msup><mo>∇</mo><mn>2</mn></msup><mo>+</mo><mi>V</mi><mrow><mo>(</mo><mi>x</mi><mo>)</mo></mrow></mrow><mo>)</mo></mrow><mi>ϕ</mi><mrow><mo>(</mo><mi>x</mi><mo>)</mo></mrow><mo>=</mo><mi>E</mi><mi>ϕ</mi><mrow><mo>(</mo><mi>x</mi><mo>)</mo></mrow></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">(7)</td>
</tr></table>
<p>where <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="inline-formula54-15353702231220665">
<math id="math61-15353702231220665" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mi>H</mi></mrow></math>
</span> represents the Hamiltonian operator, which describes the total energy of the quantum system. <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="inline-formula55-15353702231220665">
<math id="math62-15353702231220665" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mi>ϕ</mi><mrow><mo>(</mo><mi>x</mi><mo>)</mo></mrow></mrow></math>
</span> denotes the wave function, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="inline-formula56-15353702231220665">
<math id="math63-15353702231220665" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mi>V</mi><mrow><mo>(</mo><mi>x</mi><mo>)</mo></mrow></mrow></math>
</span> is a potential function, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="inline-formula57-15353702231220665">
<math id="math64-15353702231220665" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mi>E</mi></mrow></math>
</span> represents the possible eigenvalue of <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="inline-formula58-15353702231220665">
<math id="math65-15353702231220665" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mi>H</mi></mrow></math>
</span>, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="inline-formula59-15353702231220665">
<math id="math66-15353702231220665" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><msup><mo>∇</mo><mn>2</mn></msup></mrow></math>
</span> is the Laplacian operator, and <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="inline-formula60-15353702231220665">
<math id="math67-15353702231220665" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mi>δ</mi></mrow></math>
</span> is the scaling parameter of the wave function. Different values of <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="inline-formula61-15353702231220665">
<math id="math68-15353702231220665" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mi>δ</mi></mrow></math>
</span> correspond to different quantum potential energies.</p>
<p>With the known wave function, we can determine the quantum potential function <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="inline-formula62-15353702231220665">
<math id="math69-15353702231220665" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mi>V</mi><mrow><mo>(</mo><mi>x</mi><mo>)</mo></mrow></mrow></math>
</span> by solving <a href="#disp-formula7-15353702231220665" class="usa-link">equation (7)</a>, which describes the probability density function of the input data.<sup>
<a href="#bibr34-15353702231220665" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bibr34-15353702231220665">34</a>
</sup> By utilizing the Gaussian kernel, as shown in <a href="#disp-formula8-15353702231220665" class="usa-link">equation (8)</a>, for the wave function, we can solve for the potential function in a quantum system, as presented in <a href="#disp-formula9-15353702231220665" class="usa-link">equation (9)</a>.</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="disp-formula8-15353702231220665"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="math70-15353702231220665" display="block" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mi>ϕ</mi><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>x</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo><mo>=</mo><munderover><mo>∑</mo><mrow><mi>i</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><mi>n</mi></munderover><msup><mi>e</mi><mrow><mfrac><mrow><mo>−</mo><msup><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>x</mi><mo>−</mo><msub><mi>x</mi><mi>i</mi></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><mn>2</mn></msup></mrow><mrow><mn>2</mn><msup><mi>δ</mi><mn>2</mn></msup></mrow></mfrac></mrow></msup></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">(8)</td>
</tr></table>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="disp-formula9-15353702231220665"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="math71-15353702231220665" display="block" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mi>V</mi><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>x</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo><mo>=</mo><mi>E</mi><mo>+</mo><mfrac><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mfrac><mrow><msup><mi>δ</mi><mn>2</mn></msup></mrow><mn>2</mn></mfrac><mo stretchy="false">)</mo><msup><mo>∇</mo><mn>2</mn></msup><mi>ϕ</mi><mrow><mo>(</mo><mi>x</mi><mo>)</mo></mrow></mrow><mrow><mi>ϕ</mi><mrow><mo>(</mo><mi>x</mi><mo>)</mo></mrow></mrow></mfrac></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">(9)</td>
</tr></table>
<p>By substituting <a href="#disp-formula8-15353702231220665" class="usa-link">equation (8)</a> into <a href="#disp-formula9-15353702231220665" class="usa-link">equation (9)</a>, the potential function <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="inline-formula63-15353702231220665">
<math id="math72-15353702231220665" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mi>V</mi><mrow><mo>(</mo><mi>x</mi><mo>)</mo></mrow></mrow></math>
</span> can be obtained as:</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="disp-formula10-15353702231220665"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="math73-15353702231220665" display="block" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mi>V</mi><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>x</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo><mo>=</mo><mi>E</mi><mo>+</mo><mfrac><mn>1</mn><mrow><mn>2</mn><msup><mi>δ</mi><mn>2</mn></msup><mi>ϕ</mi><mrow><mo>(</mo><mi>x</mi><mo>)</mo></mrow></mrow></mfrac><munderover><mo>∑</mo><mrow><mi>i</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><mi>n</mi></munderover><msup><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>x</mi><mo>−</mo><msub><mi>x</mi><mi>i</mi></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><mn>2</mn></msup><mi>ϕ</mi><mrow><mo>(</mo><mi>x</mi><mo>)</mo></mrow></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">(10)</td>
</tr></table>
<p>Hence, the potential of each sample can be calculated precisely. Consequently, the center of the cover is determined by the sample with the minimum potential energy.</p></section><section id="section7-15353702231220665"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Quantum-based oversampling method</h3>
<p>The proposed QOSM aims to address the bias in the classification boundary by modifying the distribution of the minority class samples in the overlapping regions, thereby improving classification performance. The method involves three stages: cover construction, identification of minority covers in the overlapping area, and oversampling.</p>
<p>In the cover construction stage, the spatial distribution of the original data set is determined based on the relationship between individual samples. Both the minority- and majority-class cover sets are created, where each cover represents a group of samples with the same class label. The center of each cover is determined by selecting the sample with the minimum quantum potential energy calculated by <a href="#disp-formula10-15353702231220665" class="usa-link">equation (10)</a>.</p>
<p>In the second stage, the KNN method is employed to explore the distribution characteristics between covers at a finer granularity. QOSM leverages the cover properties to investigate the spatial relationships of covers. This analysis allows for the identification of minority class covers located in the overlapping area.</p>
<p>Finally, oversampling is applied to the samples belonging to the minority class covers identified in the second stage. This increases the sample size of the minority class in the overlapping area, resulting in a balanced data set. The technical details are presented in the following subsections.</p>
<section id="section8-15353702231220665"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">Constructing covers using sample quantum potential energy</h4>
<p>The construction of covers is based on the characteristics of the sample space. Each cover comprises two crucial parameters: the cover center and cover radius. The principle underlying cover construction is to gather more samples with the same label around the cover center, ensuring high similarity among these samples. In CCA, the cover center is selected randomly, and different choices of cover centers yield varying coverage areas. Consequently, the output of CCA is greatly influenced by the cover center selection. Improper selection of the cover center plays a critical role in the overall learning process.</p>
<p>The proposed QOSM address this issue by selecting cover centers based on quantum theory, aiming to construct cover sets that align better with the sample distribution. For all samples with known class labels in the training data set, the potential energy of each sample is calculated using the method described in previous sections. Then, for all uncovered samples with the same class label, the sample with the lowest potential energy of this class is selected as the center used to construct the cover. The remaining uncovered samples of the same class are also used to construct covers in a similar manner until all samples are covered. The details of cover construction in our method are described in Algorithm 1 (<a href="#fig3-15353702231220665" class="usa-link">Figure 3</a>).</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="fig3-15353702231220665"><h5 class="obj_head">Figure 3.</h5>

</sup></p>

</span> represents the dimension of the data set, which is the number of features, and its value is <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="inline-formula65-15353702231220665">
<math id="math76-15353702231220665" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mi>n</mi><mo>+</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow></math>

</span>.</p>
<p><a href="#fig4-15353702231220665" class="usa-link">Figure 4(a)</a> illustrates a given data set, and Algorithm 1 constructs a cover set for samples with the same label, as shown in <a href="#fig4-15353702231220665" class="usa-link">Figure 4(b)</a>. In the figure, solid circles represent the covers of minority-class samples, while dotted circles represent the covers of majority-class samples. The algorithm maps an <em>n</em>-dimensional input vector to an (<em>n</em> + 1)-dimensional space, thus representing an (<em>n</em> + 1)-dimensional input vector covering problem. Different classes of inputs are covered by distinct sets of sphere neighborhoods. For binary classification, two cover subsets, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="inline-formula68-15353702231220665">
<math id="math79-15353702231220665" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><msub><mi>C</mi><mn>0</mn></msub><mo>=</mo><mrow><mo>{</mo><mrow><msubsup><mi>C</mi><mn>0</mn><mn>1</mn></msubsup><mo>,</mo><msubsup><mi>C</mi><mn>0</mn><mn>2</mn></msubsup><mo>,</mo><mo>…</mo><mo>,</mo><msubsup><mi>C</mi><mn>0</mn><mrow><msub><mi>n</mi><mn>0</mn></msub></mrow></msubsup></mrow><mo>}</mo></mrow></mrow></math>
</span> and <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="inline-formula69-15353702231220665">
<math id="math80-15353702231220665" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><msub><mi>C</mi><mn>1</mn></msub><mo>=</mo><mrow><mo>{</mo><mrow><msubsup><mi>C</mi><mn>1</mn><mn>1</mn></msubsup><mo>,</mo><msubsup><mi>C</mi><mn>1</mn><mn>2</mn></msubsup><mo>,</mo><mo>…</mo><mo>,</mo><msubsup><mi>C</mi><mn>1</mn><mrow><msub><mi>n</mi><mn>1</mn></msub></mrow></msubsup></mrow><mo>}</mo></mrow></mrow></math>
</span>, can be constructed, corresponding to the minority and majority classes, where <em>n</em>0 and <em>n</em>1 are the number of covers in the minority and majority cover sets, respectively.</p>

</span>.</p></figcaption></figure></section><section id="section9-15353702231220665"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">Finding the minority covers in the overlapping area</h4>
<p>The second stage of the proposed method focuses on finding the minority class covers in the overlapping area. The cover construction method described previously is applied to the input data set <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="inline-formula72-15353702231220665">
<math id="math83-15353702231220665" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mi>X</mi></mrow></math>
</span>, resulting in a minority class cover set <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="inline-formula73-15353702231220665">
<math id="math84-15353702231220665" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><msub><mi>C</mi><mn>0</mn></msub></mrow></math>
</span> and a majority class cover set <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="inline-formula74-15353702231220665">
<math id="math85-15353702231220665" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><msub><mi>C</mi><mn>1</mn></msub></mrow></math>
</span>, represented by the solid line circles and dotted line circles in <a href="#fig4-15353702231220665" class="usa-link">Figure 4(c)</a>. Each cover contains essential information, such as the cover’s class label, center, radius, and samples it contains. Since the samples within each cover exhibit high similarity, we use the cover center to represent each cover. The sets of center points for the minority and majority class covers are denoted as <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="inline-formula75-15353702231220665">
<math id="math86-15353702231220665" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><msub><mrow><mi>c</mi><mi>e</mi><mi>n</mi><mi>t</mi><mi>e</mi><mi>r</mi></mrow><mn>0</mn></msub><mo>=</mo><mo>{</mo><msubsup><mrow><mi>c</mi><mi>e</mi><mi>n</mi><mi>t</mi><mi>e</mi><mi>r</mi></mrow><mrow><mn>0</mn></mrow><mn>1</mn></msubsup><mo>,</mo><msubsup><mrow><mi>c</mi><mi>e</mi><mi>n</mi><mi>t</mi><mi>e</mi><mi>r</mi></mrow><mrow><mn>0</mn></mrow><mn>2</mn></msubsup><mo>,</mo><mo>…</mo><mo>,</mo><msubsup><mrow><mi>c</mi><mi>e</mi><mi>n</mi><mi>t</mi><mi>e</mi><mi>r</mi></mrow><mrow><mn>0</mn></mrow><mrow><msub><mi>n</mi><mn>0</mn></msub></mrow></msubsup><mo>}</mo></mrow></math>
</span>= <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="inline-formula76-15353702231220665">
<math id="math87-15353702231220665" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mo>{</mo><msubsup><mrow><mi>c</mi><mi>e</mi><mi>n</mi><mi>t</mi><mi>e</mi><mi>r</mi></mrow><mrow><mn>0</mn></mrow><mn>1</mn></msubsup><mo>,</mo><msubsup><mrow><mi>c</mi><mi>e</mi><mi>n</mi><mi>t</mi><mi>e</mi><mi>r</mi></mrow><mrow><mn>0</mn></mrow><mn>2</mn></msubsup><mo>,</mo><mo>…</mo><mo>,</mo><msubsup><mrow><mi>c</mi><mi>e</mi><mi>n</mi><mi>t</mi><mi>e</mi><mi>r</mi></mrow><mrow><mn>0</mn></mrow><mrow><msub><mi>n</mi><mn>0</mn></msub></mrow></msubsup><mo>}</mo></mrow></math>
</span> and <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="inline-formula77-15353702231220665">
<math id="math88-15353702231220665" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><msub><mrow><mi>c</mi><mi>e</mi><mi>n</mi><mi>t</mi><mi>e</mi><mi>r</mi></mrow><mn>1</mn></msub><mo>=</mo><mo>{</mo><msubsup><mrow><mi>c</mi><mi>e</mi><mi>n</mi><mi>t</mi><mi>e</mi><mi>r</mi></mrow><mrow><mn>1</mn></mrow><mn>1</mn></msubsup><mo>,</mo><msubsup><mrow><mi>c</mi><mi>e</mi><mi>n</mi><mi>t</mi><mi>e</mi><mi>r</mi></mrow><mrow><mn>1</mn></mrow><mn>2</mn></msubsup><mo>,</mo><mo>…</mo><mo>,</mo><msubsup><mrow><mi>c</mi><mi>e</mi><mi>n</mi><mi>t</mi><mi>e</mi><mi>r</mi></mrow><mrow><mn>1</mn></mrow><mrow><msub><mi>n</mi><mn>1</mn></msub></mrow></msubsup><mo>}</mo></mrow></math>
</span>, respectively. Next, we employ the KNN method to explore the relationship between each pair of covers and the spatial distribution characteristics among them. Specifically, we examine the number of majority class covers <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="inline-formula78-15353702231220665">
<math id="math89-15353702231220665" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><msub><mi>n</mi><mi>i</mi></msub><mo>_</mo><mi>m</mi><mi>a</mi><mi>j</mi></mrow></math>
</span> among the <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="inline-formula79-15353702231220665">
<math id="math90-15353702231220665" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">KNNs</mi></mrow></math>
</span> of a minority class cover <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="inline-formula80-15353702231220665">
<math id="math91-15353702231220665" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><msubsup><mi>C</mi><mn>0</mn><mi>i</mi></msubsup></mrow></math>
</span> to determine the presence of minority covers within the overlapping area. By comparing the values of <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="inline-formula81-15353702231220665">
<math id="math92-15353702231220665" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><msub><mi>n</mi><mi>i</mi></msub><mo>_</mo><mi>m</mi><mi>a</mi><mi>j</mi></mrow></math>
</span> and <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="inline-formula82-15353702231220665">
<math id="math93-15353702231220665" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mi>k</mi></mrow></math>
</span> and considering a threshold <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="inline-formula83-15353702231220665">
<math id="math94-15353702231220665" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mi>α</mi></mrow></math>
</span>, (e.g., <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="inline-formula84-15353702231220665">
<math id="math95-15353702231220665" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mi>α</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>0</mn><mo>.</mo><mn>3</mn><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></math>
</span>, we can obtain three difference cases:</p>
<ol id="list6-15353702231220665" class="list" style="list-style-type:decimal">
<li><p>If <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="inline-formula85-15353702231220665">
<math id="math96-15353702231220665" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><msub><mi>n</mi><mi>i</mi></msub><mo>_</mo><mi>m</mi><mi>a</mi><mi>j</mi><mo>&gt;</mo><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mn>1</mn><mo>−</mo><mi>α</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo><mi>k</mi></mrow></math>
</span>, indicating that the majority of nearest neighbors of the minority cover <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="inline-formula86-15353702231220665">
<math id="math97-15353702231220665" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><msubsup><mi>C</mi><mn>0</mn><mi>i</mi></msubsup></mrow></math>
</span> are majority class covers, then this minority cover is situated within the majority class area. Since most of its neighbors are majority covers, the minority cover may be considered as noise or a small unconnected set. Therefore, it cannot be located at the classification boundary and is not selected for oversampling.</p></li>
<li><p>If <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="inline-formula87-15353702231220665">
<math id="math98-15353702231220665" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><msub><mi>n</mi><mi>j</mi></msub><mo>_</mo><mi>m</mi><mi>a</mi><mi>j</mi><mo>&lt;</mo><mi>α</mi><mi>k</mi></mrow></math>
</span>, indicating that most of the nearest neighbors of the cover <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="inline-formula88-15353702231220665">
<math id="math99-15353702231220665" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><msubsup><mi>C</mi><mn>0</mn><mi>i</mi></msubsup></mrow></math>
</span> are minority class covers. It can be inferred that the cover is situated within the area of the minority class and is not selected for oversampling.</p></li>
<li><p>If <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="inline-formula89-15353702231220665">
<math id="math100-15353702231220665" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mi>α</mi><mi>k</mi><mo>≤</mo><msub><mi>n</mi><mi>j</mi></msub><mo>_</mo><mi>m</mi><mi>a</mi><mi>j</mi><mo>≤</mo><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mn>1</mn><mo>−</mo><mi>α</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo><mi>k</mi><mo>,</mo></mrow></math>
</span> the minority class cover is located in an area where samples belong to both the minority and majority classes. This suggests that the cover is situated within the overlapping area and is a good candidate for oversampling.</p></li>
</ol>
<p>To illustrate the process of identifying minority covers within the overlapping area, let’s consider an example. For a minority class cover <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="inline-formula90-15353702231220665">
<math id="math101-15353702231220665" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><msubsup><mi>C</mi><mn>0</mn><mi>i</mi></msubsup></mrow></math>
</span>, we identify its <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="inline-formula91-15353702231220665">
<math id="math102-15353702231220665" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mi>k</mi></mrow></math>
</span> (set <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="inline-formula92-15353702231220665">
<math id="math103-15353702231220665" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mi>k</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>4</mn></mrow></math>
</span>) nearest neighbors <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="inline-formula93-15353702231220665">
<math id="math104-15353702231220665" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><msubsup><mi>c</mi><mn>0</mn><mi>a</mi></msubsup></mrow></math>
</span>, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="inline-formula94-15353702231220665">
<math id="math105-15353702231220665" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><msubsup><mi>c</mi><mn>1</mn><mi>b</mi></msubsup></mrow></math>
</span>, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="inline-formula95-15353702231220665">
<math id="math106-15353702231220665" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><msubsup><mi>c</mi><mn>1</mn><mi>c</mi></msubsup></mrow></math>
</span>, and <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="inline-formula96-15353702231220665">
<math id="math107-15353702231220665" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><msubsup><mi>c</mi><mn>1</mn><mi>d</mi></msubsup></mrow></math>
</span>. Among these neighbors, there are three majority class covers (represented by the red dotted line circle), which corresponds to Case 3, satisfying <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="inline-formula97-15353702231220665">
<math id="math108-15353702231220665" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mo>(</mo><mrow><mn>1</mn><mo>−</mo><mn>0</mn><mo>.</mo><mn>3</mn></mrow><mo>)</mo></mrow><mi>k</mi><mo>≤</mo><msub><mi>n</mi><mrow><mi>j</mi><mi>m</mi><mi>a</mi><mi>j</mi></mrow></msub><mo>≤</mo><mn>0</mn><mo>.</mo><mn>3</mn><mi>k</mi></mrow></math>
</span>. In this case, the cover <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="inline-formula98-15353702231220665">
<math id="math109-15353702231220665" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><msubsup><mi>C</mi><mn>0</mn><mi>i</mi></msubsup></mrow></math>
</span> is situated close to the decision boundary between the minority and majority classes. The majority of samples within this cover are in the overlapping area, and these minority samples will be utilized for oversampling.</p></section><section id="section10-15353702231220665"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">Oversampling in the overlapping area</h4>
<p>The proposed QOSM incorporates oversampling of the minority class covers in the overlapping area. The goal is to ensure that the synthetic samples are distributed evenly within the overlapping region, thereby enhancing the representation of the minority class and expanding its area. The oversampling process follows these steps:</p>
<ol id="list7-15353702231220665" class="list" style="list-style-type:decimal">
<li><p>Calculate centroids, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="inline-formula99-15353702231220665">
<math id="math110-15353702231220665" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><msub><mi>x</mi><mrow><mi>m</mi><mi>e</mi><mi>a</mi><mi>n</mi></mrow></msub></mrow></math>
</span> for all the minority samples in the overlapping area. This centroid represents the central position within the overlapping region, as illustrated in <a href="#fig5-15353702231220665" class="usa-link">Figure 5</a>.</p></li>
<li><p>Generate synthetic samples, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="inline-formula100-15353702231220665">
<math id="math111-15353702231220665" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><msub><mi>x</mi><mrow><mi>s</mi><mi>y</mi><mi>n</mi><mi>t</mi><mi>h</mi><mi>e</mi><mi>t</mi><mi>i</mi><mi>c</mi></mrow></msub></mrow></math>
</span>, using linear interpolation between each sample,<span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="inline-formula101-15353702231220665">
<math id="math112-15353702231220665" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><msub><mi>x</mi><mi>i</mi></msub></mrow></math>
</span>, and the centroid <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="inline-formula102-15353702231220665">
<math id="math113-15353702231220665" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><msub><mi>x</mi><mrow><mi>m</mi><mi>e</mi><mi>a</mi><mi>n</mi></mrow></msub></mrow></math>
</span>. Specifically, the synthetic sample is calculated by <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="inline-formula103-15353702231220665">
<math id="math114-15353702231220665" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><msub><mi>x</mi><mrow><mi>s</mi><mi>y</mi><mi>n</mi><mi>t</mi><mi>h</mi><mi>e</mi><mi>t</mi><mi>i</mi><mi>c</mi></mrow></msub><mo>=</mo><msub><mi>x</mi><mrow><mi>m</mi><mi>e</mi><mi>a</mi><mi>n</mi></mrow></msub><mo>+</mo><mi>θ</mi><mo>⋅</mo><mrow><mo>(</mo><mrow><msub><mi>x</mi><mi>i</mi></msub><mo>−</mo><msub><mi>x</mi><mrow><mi>m</mi><mi>e</mi><mi>a</mi><mi>n</mi></mrow></msub></mrow><mo>)</mo></mrow></mrow></math>
</span>, where <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="inline-formula104-15353702231220665">
<math id="math115-15353702231220665" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mi>θ</mi></mrow></math>
</span> is a random number ranging between 0 and 1. This process is similar to the SMOTE algorithm,<sup>
<a href="#bibr9-15353702231220665" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bibr9-15353702231220665">9</a>
</sup> but with a distinction. In QOSM, synthetic samples are generated between a minority sample and the centroid of all minority samples in the overlapping area, instead of generating them between pairs of individual minority samples. This approach helps mitigate the impact of noisy data and reduces the likelihood of creating mislabeled synthetic samples.</p></li>
</ol>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="fig5-15353702231220665"><h5 class="obj_head">Figure 5.</h5>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><img class="graphic" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/a261/10854475/a9095953d3fb/10.1177_15353702231220665-fig5.jpg" loading="lazy" height="585" width="643" alt="Figure 5."></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/fig5-15353702231220665/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>The process of oversampling in the overlapping area.</p></figcaption></figure><p>The number of synthetic samples generated for each minority sample is a parameter that can be determined on a case-by-case basis, considering factors, such as data characteristics (e.g. IR and maximum Fisher’s discriminant ratio <em>maxF</em>) and the desired IR after the oversampling process.</p>
<p>It is important to note that QOSM focuses on generating synthetic samples specifically in the overlapping area. By applying oversampling, the total number of samples in the minority class increases, which tends to balance the training set. Moreover, the distribution of the minority class within the overlapping region is altered. After addressing the issue of imbalance and overlap using QOSM, the traditional classification algorithms can be employed to build the models for further classification and prediction.</p>
<p>Based on the principle of minimizing overall error, the decision boundary will shift to some extent toward the majority class area, aiming to achieve higher accuracy in predicting the minority class samples in the overlapping area. QOSM seeks to achieve an improved balance between the classification of the minority and the majority class, thereby improving the overall classification performance. The oversampling of the minority class samples in the overlapping area increases the discriminative capability of the classifiers to some extent. The pseudo-code for the QOSM algorithm is presented in Algorithm 2 (<a href="#fig6-15353702231220665" class="usa-link">Figure 6</a>).</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="fig6-15353702231220665"><h5 class="obj_head">Figure 6.</h5>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><img class="graphic" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/a261/10854475/af48c8f623ad/10.1177_15353702231220665-fig6.jpg" loading="lazy" height="660" width="675" alt="Figure 6."></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/fig6-15353702231220665/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Algorithm of QOSM.</p></figcaption></figure></section></section></section><section id="section11-15353702231220665"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">Results</h2>
<section id="section12-15353702231220665"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Data sets</h3>
<p>To validate the effectiveness of our proposed QOSM, we conducted experiments on 10 imbalanced and overlapped data sets from the KEEL repository website (<a href="https://sci2s.ugr.es/keel/imbalanced.php" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">https://sci2s.ugr.es/keel/imbalanced.php</a>). These data sets are widely used for evaluating the performance of imbalanced classification algorithms. <a href="#table1-15353702231220665" class="usa-link">Table 1</a> provides a summary of these data sets, including their Abbreviation (Abb), Instances (Ins), Features (Fea), Minority Class Sample Proportion (<em>%min</em>), Majority Class Sample Proportion (<em>%maj</em>), <em>IR</em>, and <em>maxF</em> (the degree of overlap). Data sets are arranged in ascending order by the <em>maxF</em> value. A smaller <em>maxF</em> value indicates a higher degree of overlap. All 10 data sets exhibit high levels of class imbalance with <em>IR</em> values greater than 9, and significant overlap with <em>maxF</em> values less than 4.5. Among these data sets, only one exhibits a relatively low overlap, with a <em>maxF</em> value greater than 2.5, while the remaining nine data sets have <em>maxF</em> values below 2.5.</p>
