<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="table/table1-15353702231220665/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div></section><p>In <a href="#table1-15353702231220665" class="usa-link">Table 1</a>, <em>maxF</em> is a measurement used to quantify the degree of overlap between classes.<sup><a href="#bibr36-15353702231220665" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bibr36-15353702231220665">36</a>,<a href="#bibr24-15353702231220665" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bibr24-15353702231220665">24</a></sup> It calculates the maximum discriminant ratio achieved by Fisher’s method across all the dimensions of an object. Fisher’s discriminant ratio for a single dimension is defined as:</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="disp-formula12-15353702231220665"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="math116-15353702231220665" display="block" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><msub><mi>f</mi><mi>i</mi></msub><mo>=</mo><mfrac><mrow><msup><mrow><mrow><mo>(</mo><mrow><msub><mi>μ</mi><mn>0</mn></msub><mo>−</mo><msub><mi>μ</mi><mn>1</mn></msub></mrow><mo>)</mo></mrow></mrow><mn>2</mn></msup></mrow><mrow><msup><mrow><msub><mi>σ</mi><mn>0</mn></msub></mrow><mn>2</mn></msup><mo>+</mo><msup><mrow><msub><mi>σ</mi><mn>1</mn></msub></mrow><mn>2</mn></msup></mrow></mfrac></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">(12)</td>
</tr></table>
<p>where <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="inline-formula105-15353702231220665">
<math id="math117-15353702231220665" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><msub><mi>f</mi><mi>i</mi></msub></mrow></math>
</span> represents the Fisher’s discriminant ratio of dimension <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="inline-formula106-15353702231220665">
<math id="math118-15353702231220665" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mi>i</mi></mrow></math>
</span>, measuring the separability of the two classes in that dimension. <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="inline-formula107-15353702231220665">
<math id="math119-15353702231220665" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><msub><mi>μ</mi><mn>0</mn></msub></mrow></math>
</span>, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="inline-formula108-15353702231220665">
<math id="math120-15353702231220665" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><msub><mi>μ</mi><mn>1</mn></msub></mrow></math>
</span>, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="inline-formula109-15353702231220665">
<math id="math121-15353702231220665" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><msup><mrow><msub><mi>σ</mi><mn>0</mn></msub></mrow><mn>2</mn></msup></mrow></math>
</span>, and <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="inline-formula110-15353702231220665">
<math id="math122-15353702231220665" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><msup><mrow><msub><mi>σ</mi><mn>1</mn></msub></mrow><mn>2</mn></msup></mrow></math>
</span> denote the means and variances of classes 0 and 1 in dimension <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="inline-formula111-15353702231220665">
<math id="math123-15353702231220665" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mi>i</mi></mrow></math>
</span>, respectively. The degree of class overlap can be evaluated by <em>maxF</em>, the maximum <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="inline-formula112-15353702231220665">
<math id="math124-15353702231220665" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><msub><mi>f</mi><mi>i</mi></msub></mrow></math>
</span> across all the feature dimensions:</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="disp-formula13-15353702231220665"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="math125-15353702231220665" display="block" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mi>m</mi><mi>a</mi><mi>x</mi><mi>F</mi><mo>=</mo><mi>m</mi><mi>a</mi><mi>x</mi><mrow><mo>{</mo><mrow><msub><mi>f</mi><mi>i</mi></msub></mrow><mo>}</mo></mrow></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">(13)</td>
</tr></table>
<p>where <em>maxF</em> ranges from 0 to infinity, and a lower <em>maxF</em> value indicates a higher degree of overlap in the data set.</p></section><section id="section13-15353702231220665"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Evaluation metrics</h3>
<p>We employed three evaluation metrics to assess the performance of our proposed algorithm: <em>F</em>-measure, area under the receiver operator characteristic (ROC) curve (AUC), and G-mean. These metrics can be derived from the confusion matrix where the positive and negative classes represent the minority and majority classes, respectively. In the confusion matrix, TP represents the number of positive samples that are correctly classified as positive, FN represents the number of positive samples that are misclassified as negative, FP represents the number of negative samples misclassified as positive, and TN represents the number of negative samples that are correctly classified as negative. The evaluation metrics true positive rate (TPR), true negative rate (TNR), accuracy, precision, recall, <em>F</em>-measure, and G-mean are defined by <a href="#disp-formula14-15353702231220665" class="usa-link">equations (14)</a> to (20), respectively.</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="disp-formula14-15353702231220665"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="math126-15353702231220665" display="block" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mi>T</mi><mi>P</mi><mi>R</mi><mo>=</mo><mfrac><mrow><mi>T</mi><mi>P</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>T</mi><mi>P</mi><mo>+</mo><mi>F</mi><mi>N</mi></mrow></mfrac></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">(14)</td>
</tr></table>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="disp-formula15-15353702231220665"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="math127-15353702231220665" display="block" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mi>T</mi><mi>N</mi><mi>R</mi><mo>=</mo><mfrac><mrow><mi>T</mi><mi>N</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>T</mi><mi>N</mi><mo>+</mo><mi>F</mi><mi>P</mi></mrow></mfrac></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">(15)</td>
</tr></table>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="disp-formula16-15353702231220665"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="math128-15353702231220665" display="block" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mi>A</mi><mi>c</mi><mi>c</mi><mi>u</mi><mi>r</mi><mi>a</mi><mi>c</mi><mi>y</mi><mo>=</mo><mfrac><mrow><mi>T</mi><mi>P</mi><mo>+</mo><mi>T</mi><mi>N</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>T</mi><mi>P</mi><mo>+</mo><mi>T</mi><mi>N</mi><mo>+</mo><mi>F</mi><mi>P</mi><mo>+</mo><mi>F</mi><mi>N</mi></mrow></mfrac></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">(16)</td>
</tr></table>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="disp-formula17-15353702231220665"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="math129-15353702231220665" display="block" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mi>P</mi><mi>r</mi><mi>e</mi><mi>c</mi><mi>i</mi><mi>s</mi><mi>i</mi><mi>o</mi><mi>n</mi><mo>=</mo><mfrac><mrow><mi>T</mi><mi>P</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>T</mi><mi>P</mi><mo>+</mo><mi>F</mi><mi>P</mi></mrow></mfrac></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">(17)</td>
</tr></table>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="disp-formula18-15353702231220665"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="math130-15353702231220665" display="block" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mi>R</mi><mi>e</mi><mi>c</mi><mi>a</mi><mi>l</mi><mi>l</mi><mo>=</mo><mfrac><mrow><mi>T</mi><mi>P</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>T</mi><mi>P</mi><mo>+</mo><mi>F</mi><mi>N</mi></mrow></mfrac></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">(18)</td>
</tr></table>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="disp-formula19-15353702231220665"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="math131-15353702231220665" display="block" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mi>F</mi><mo>-</mo><mi>m</mi><mi>e</mi><mi>a</mi><mi>s</mi><mi>u</mi><mi>r</mi><mi>e</mi><mo>=</mo><mfrac><mrow><mrow><mo>(</mo><mrow><mn>1</mn><mo>+</mo><msup><mi>β</mi><mn>2</mn></msup></mrow><mo>)</mo></mrow><mi>R</mi><mi>e</mi><mi>c</mi><mi>a</mi><mi>l</mi><mi>l</mi><mo>×</mo><mi>P</mi><mi>r</mi><mi>e</mi><mi>c</mi><mi>i</mi><mi>s</mi><mi>i</mi><mi>o</mi><mi>n</mi></mrow><mrow><msup><mi>β</mi><mn>2</mn></msup><mo>×</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">Re</mi><mi>c</mi><mi>a</mi><mi>l</mi><mi>l</mi><mo>+</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">Pr</mi><mi>e</mi><mi>c</mi><mi>i</mi><mi>s</mi><mi>i</mi><mi>o</mi><mi>n</mi></mrow></mfrac><mi>β</mi><mo>≥</mo><mn>0</mn></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">(19)</td>
</tr></table>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="disp-formula20-15353702231220665"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="math132-15353702231220665" display="block" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mi>G</mi><mo>−</mo><mi>m</mi><mi>e</mi><mi>a</mi><mi>n</mi><mo>=</mo><msqrt><mrow><mi>T</mi><mi>P</mi><mi>R</mi><mo>×</mo><mi>T</mi><mi>N</mi><mi>R</mi></mrow></msqrt></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">(20)</td>
</tr></table>
<p>Generally, <em>Precision</em> and <em>Recall</em> exhibit an inverse relationship, where increasing one may come at the cost of reducing the other. <em>F</em>-measure provides a comprehensive measure by combining <em>Precision</em> and <em>Recall</em>, where <em>β</em> is the parameter used to adjust their relative importance. In our experiments,
<em>β</em>
is set to 1 making <em>F</em>-measure the harmonic mean of <em>Recall</em> and <em>Precision</em>. In addition, we employed G-mean as an evaluation metric, which represents the geometric mean of the classification accuracy of positive and negative samples. The ROC curve can be drawn by plotting the TPR against the false positive rate (FPR) at various threshold settings, where TPR and FPR can be calculated based on the confusion matrix. The ROC value, ranging from 0 to 1.0, indicates the overall performance of an algorithm. A higher ROC value signifies superior overall performance.</p></section><section id="section14-15353702231220665"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Comparison experiments and analysis</h3>
<p>We conducted three sets of experiments to evaluate the effectiveness of our proposed method. The first set aimed to validate the performance improvement of three traditional base classification algorithms when QOSM is used, as illustrated in <a href="#fig7-15353702231220665" class="usa-link">Figure 7</a> and reported in <a href="#table2-15353702231220665" class="usa-link">Table 2</a>. The second set of experiments aimed to verify the effectiveness of the improved constructive covering algorithm based on quantum potential energy, and the results are presented in <a href="#table3-15353702231220665" class="usa-link">Table 3</a>. Finally, we compared the performance of our QOSM with three other oversampling methods, and the results on three base classifiers (SVM, KNN, and BN) are reported in <a href="#table4-15353702231220665" class="usa-link">Tables 4</a> to <a href="#fig6-15353702231220665" class="usa-link">6</a>.</p>

<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="table/table4-15353702231220665/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<div class="tw-foot p">
<div class="fn" id="table-fn4-15353702231220665"><p>AUC: area under the curve; SVM: support vector machines; QOSM: quantum-based oversampling method; IPF: iterative-partitioning filter.</p></div>
<div class="fn" id="table-fn5-15353702231220665"><p>The bold values represent the best results achieved among the four oversampling methods for each dataset and evaluation metric.</p></div>
</div></section><p>It is important to note that, in QOSM, the step of finding the <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="inline-formula113-15353702231220665">
<math id="math133-15353702231220665" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">KNNs</mi></mrow></math>
</span> of each minority class cover is crucial, where the value of <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="inline-formula114-15353702231220665">
<math id="math134-15353702231220665" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mi>k</mi></mrow></math>
</span> needs to be determined empirically based on the data set. However, determining the optimal value of k is not the primary focus of this article. Instead, we conducted an additional comparative experiment to find an acceptable <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="inline-formula115-15353702231220665">
<math id="math135-15353702231220665" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mi>k</mi></mrow></math>
</span> value. In this experiment, we set <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="inline-formula116-15353702231220665">
<math id="math136-15353702231220665" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mi>k</mi></mrow></math>
</span> to values of 3, 4, 5, and 6 respectively, and evaluated the performance using the accuracy metric. The experimental results show that the best results were obtained when <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="inline-formula117-15353702231220665">
<math id="math137-15353702231220665" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mi>k</mi></mrow></math>
</span> is set to 4. Therefore, three groups of experiments were conducted using this setting.</p>
<p>Our work primarily focuses on the oversampling methods rather than the classification algorithms themselves. Therefore, we utilized three widely used classifiers, namely, SVM,<sup>
<a href="#bibr37-15353702231220665" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bibr37-15353702231220665">37</a>
</sup> KNNs, and NB,<sup>
<a href="#bibr38-15353702231220665" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bibr38-15353702231220665">38</a>
</sup> as the base classifiers in our experiments. These classifiers were implemented using the scikit-learn machine learning library in Python, with their default parameter settings. In our experiments, the data sets are partitioned into training and testing sets at an 80–20% ratio, and five-fold cross-validation was employed for model selection. The models were evaluated using <em>F</em>-measure, AUC, and G-mean metrics. The results of three sets of experiments are presented and analyzed in detail in the following three subsections.</p>
<section id="section15-15353702231220665"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">Classification performance with and without QOSM</h4>
<p>To demonstrate the effectiveness of QOSM, we applied it to oversample 10 highly imbalanced and overlapped data sets. Then, we used three base classifiers to classify the oversampled data and compared the results with the classification performance on the original data sets without utilizing QOSM. <a href="#table2-15353702231220665" class="usa-link">Table 2</a> reports the average <em>F</em>-measure, AUC, and G-mean values, along with their corresponding standard deviation, obtained by three traditional classifiers with and without QOSM on the 10 data sets. It is evident that SVM performs poorly when not using QOSM, with an <em>F</em>-measure of only 0.047 and an AUC of 0.5145. This demonstrates the sensitivity of SVM to data sets that are both imbalanced and overlapped, as the support vectors in SVM struggle to be determined within the overlapping area. However, when QOSM is applied, SVM’s <em>F</em>-measure and AUC significantly improve to 0.3998 and 0.8214, respectively. The results indicate that, after QOSM oversampling, the average <em>F</em>-measure, G-mean, and AUC increased across all three classifiers. Specifically, for KNN, the average <em>F</em>-measure, G-mean, and AUC improved by 18.38, 40.69, and 17.14%. Using SVM, the corresponding improvements were 35.28, 73.34, and 30.68%, whereas for NB, they were 7.85, 18.38, and 11.12%, respectively. It is important to note that these improvements were observed even when using the same classifiers and parameter settings in both cases. <a href="#table2-15353702231220665" class="usa-link">Table 2</a> also reveals that the standard deviations of our method are slightly higher than those of the classifiers without QOSM on the original data. This can be attributed to the poor ability of the base classifiers to identify minority class samples in these data sets, as depicted in <a href="#fig7-15353702231220665" class="usa-link">Figure 7</a>. Consequently, the evaluation results on each data set are relatively poor, resulting in smaller standard deviations.</p>
<p>Experimental results for each combination of data set and base classifier are depicted in <a href="#fig7-15353702231220665" class="usa-link">Figure 7</a>. The three sub-figures illustrate the results of <em>F</em>-measure, AUC, and G-mean results obtained by the three base classifiers with the default parameters on the 10 data sets, respectively. The solid lines represent the classification results using QOSM and dashed lines represent the results without using QOSM. It is evident that the QOSM significantly improves the classification performance of the three traditional classifiers on all ten data sets. This improvement can be attributed to the capability of the proposed QOSM in effectively handling the issues of class imbalance and overlap present in the data sets.</p></section><section id="section16-15353702231220665"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">Comparison between QOSM and random oversampling method</h4>
<p>We conducted experiments to compare the classification performance between QOSM and random oversampling method (ROSM). The key difference between QOSM and ROSM lies in the method of selecting cover centers. While ROSM randomly selects cover centers, QOSM utilizes quantum potential theory for cover center selection.</p>
<p><a href="#table3-15353702231220665" class="usa-link">Table 3</a> presents the comparison results of each evaluation metric using SVM, KNN and NB classifiers. “Average” values represent the mean performance across 10 data sets. The results in <a href="#table3-15353702231220665" class="usa-link">Table 3</a> demonstrate that QOSM achieved superior performance compared with ROSM on all 10 data sets. The average values of all three metrics with QOSM were consistently higher than those with ROSM for all three classifiers and across all 10 data sets. It is noted that the <em>F</em>-measure values with ROSM were very low for several data sets (e.g. Ww37, Y4, and Y6), indicating that only a few positive samples were correctly classified. In contrast, QOSM significantly improved the classification performance, and the base classifiers achieved better results when combined with QOSM.</p>
<p>Although both methods performed poorly on the A2010 data set due to its extremely high IR (IR = 72.46), QOSM outperformed ROSM. These experimental results demonstrate that the proposed oversampling method based on the constructive covering algorithm and quantum potential theory is effective. By optimizing cover center selection and generating covers that align more with the original sample distribution, QOSM better defines the decision boundary through oversampling in the overlapping region, leading to improved classification accuracy.</p></section><section id="section17-15353702231220665"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">Comparison of QOSM with other oversampling methods</h4>
<p>We compared the performance of our proposed QOSM with three state-of-the-art oversampling algorithms: Polynom-fit-SMOTE (Poly),<sup>
<a href="#bibr39-15353702231220665" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bibr39-15353702231220665">39</a>
</sup> ProWsyn (Pro),<sup>
<a href="#bibr40-15353702231220665" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bibr40-15353702231220665">40</a>
</sup> and SMOTE-IPF (IPF).<sup>
<a href="#bibr41-15353702231220665" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bibr41-15353702231220665">41</a>
</sup> Polynom-fit-SMOTE focuses on improving the TPR while maintaining a reasonable TNR by employing polynomial fitting functions for oversampling the minority class. ProWsyn introduces a synthetic oversampling algorithm that assigns appropriate weight values to minority samples based on proximity information, ensuring a balanced distribution of synthetic samples. SMOTE-IPF extends the SMOTE algorithm by incorporating an iterative ensemble-based noise filter called Iterative-Partitioning Filter (IPF) to address challenges posed by noisy and borderline examples in imbalanced data sets. In this discussion, we refer to these algorithms as Poly, Pro, and IPF, respectively. These three algorithms have demonstrated superior performance in Kovács.<sup>
<a href="#bibr42-15353702231220665" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bibr42-15353702231220665">42</a>
</sup> The parameter settings for these algorithms in our experiments are the default values provided in the scikit-learn library for Python.</p>
<p>The evaluation results for <em>F</em>-measure, AUC, and G-mean corresponding to QOSM, Poly, Pro and IPF on the 10 data sets using SVM, KNN, and NB classifiers are reported in <a href="#table4-15353702231220665" class="usa-link">Tables 4</a>, <a href="#table5-15353702231220665" class="usa-link">5</a> and <a href="#table6-15353702231220665" class="usa-link">6</a>, respectively. The best results achieved by each oversampling method on each data set are highlighted in bold. The average values for different metrics on 10 data sets are provided in the last row of each table. Observing the results in <a href="#table4-15353702231220665" class="usa-link">Tables 4</a> through 6, it is evident that when using the SVM classifier, QOSM outperformed Poly, Pro, and IPF on 7 out of 10 data sets for all three evaluation measures. In fact, QOSM combined with SVM achieved better performance than the Pro and IPF oversampling methods on all 10 data sets, as shown in <a href="#table4-15353702231220665" class="usa-link">Table 4</a>. When using KNN and NB, QOSM also exhibited better performance than the other three oversampling methods on most data sets, as indicated by all three evaluation measures.</p>

<div class="tw-foot p">
<div class="fn" id="table-fn8-15353702231220665"><p>AUC: area under the curve; NB: naive Bayes classifier; QOSM: quantum-based oversampling method; IPF: iterative-partitioning filter.</p></div>
<div class="fn" id="table-fn9-15353702231220665"><p>The bold values represent the best results achieved among the four oversampling methods for each dataset and evaluation metric.</p></div>
</div></section><p>The average values of <em>F</em>-measure, AUC, and G-mean across the 10 data sets are illustrated in <a href="#fig7-15353702231220665" class="usa-link">Figure 7</a>. The results demonstrate that QOSM consistently outperformed the other three methods for all three metrics when the SVM and NB classifiers were used. QOSM achieved higher average performance in terms of <em>F</em>-measure and AUC metrics, while its performance in terms of G-mean is slightly lower but still comparable when using the KNN classifier. These findings indicate that QOSM exhibits superior performance across multiple data sets and is less affected by different classifiers, highlighting its good generalization ability and potential for handling the imbalance and overlapping issues that exist in many real-world data sets.</p></section><section id="section18-15353702231220665"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">Significance test</h4>
<p>To determine the significance of the differences between QOSM and the other three compared oversampling methods, we conducted statistical tests using the Friedman test.<sup>
<a href="#bibr43-15353702231220665" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bibr43-15353702231220665">43</a>
</sup> The Friedman test compares the mean ranks of all the algorithms across the experimental data sets. The result of the Friedman test for each combination of the metrics and the classifiers are presented in <a href="#table7-15353702231220665" class="usa-link">Table 7</a>.</p>
<section class="tw xbox font-sm" id="table7-15353702231220665"><h5 class="obj_head">Table 7.</h5>

<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="table/table7-15353702231220665/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<div class="tw-foot p">
<div class="fn" id="table-fn10-15353702231220665"><p>AUC: area under the curve; SVM: support vector machines; KNN: k-nearest neighbor; NB: naive Bayes classifier.</p></div>
<div class="fn" id="table-fn11-15353702231220665"><p>The bold font indicates that the <em>F</em>-value is greater than the critical value of 2.96.</p></div>
</div></section><p>With a significance level of <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="inline-formula118-15353702231220665">
<math id="math138-15353702231220665" display="inline" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mi>α</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>0</mn><mo>.</mo><mn>05</mn></mrow></math>
</span>, and considering 4 algorithms and 10 data sets in the experiments, the critical value of the Friedman test is 2.96. <a href="#table7-15353702231220665" class="usa-link">Table 7</a> reveals that the <em>F</em>-values obtained with the SVM and NB classifiers are significantly higher than 2.96. This indicates that the null-hypothesis, which assumes that all the compared algorithms perform similarly, can be rejected. In other words, there are significant differences among these algorithms. However, for the KNN classifier, the <em>F</em>-values of AUC and G-mean are lower than 2.96, suggesting that while QOSM performs better than the compared algorithms, the difference is not statistically significant. Based on these results, we can conclude that the null hypothesis is rejected for almost all the compared oversampling methods at a significance level of α = 0.05. This indicates that the proposed method QOSM outperforms the other three methods in terms of statistical significance in most cases we tested.</p></section><section id="section19-15353702231220665"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">More discussion</h4>
<p>The results presented in <a href="#table3-15353702231220665" class="usa-link">Table 3</a> demonstrate that QOSM systematically improves classification performance, and this improvement is statistically significant. It is interesting to explore the impact of the <em>IR</em> and degree of overlapping (<em>maxF</em>) on the classification performance improvement. <a href="#table1-15353702231220665" class="usa-link">Table 1</a> provides <em>IR</em> and <em>maxF</em> values for ten data sets. Previous research, including our own work,<sup>
<a href="#bibr3-15353702231220665" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bibr3-15353702231220665">3</a>
</sup> has shown that <em>F</em>-measure is a better metric for evaluating classification performance on imbalanced data compared to AUC, and this has been acknowledged by other researchers.<sup>
<a href="#bibr44-15353702231220665" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bibr44-15353702231220665">44</a>
</sup> Therefore, in this analysis, we use <em>F</em>-measure to investigate the influence of <em>IR</em> on classification accuracy. From <a href="#table3-15353702231220665" class="usa-link">Table 3</a>, we define the performance <em>F<sub>diff</sub></em> as the difference of <em>F</em>-measure values obtained by QOSM and ROSM for each classifier. We then compute the correlation coefficient (<em>r)</em> between <em>F<sub>diff</sub></em>, <em>IR</em>, and <em>maxF</em>. The correlation coefficients between <em>F<sub>diff</sub></em> and IR are 0.371, 0.004, -0.266 for the classifiers SVM, KNN and NB, respectively. It is important to note that the data characteristics of the ten data sets are diverse, and the IR varies significantly, ranging from 9 to 72. Therefore, we observe no strong correlation between <em>F<sub>diff</sub></em> and IR. On the other hand, the correlation coefficients between <em>F<sub>diff</sub></em> and <em>maxF</em> are 0.674, 0.673 and 0.637 for SVM, KNN, and NB classifiers, respectively. These values indicate a strong positive correlation between <em>F<sub>diff</sub></em> and <em>maxF</em>. Notably, the <em>maxF</em> values ranges from 0.3487 to 4.1976, as shown in <a href="#table1-15353702231220665" class="usa-link">Table 1</a>, suggesting that a higher degree of overlapping leads to a more significant improvement in classification. These findings indicate that QOSM is more effective in addressing the issues of overlap issue rather than the issue of imbalance.</p>
<p>However, there are serval limitations in the current work. QOSM has only been tested on ten diverse data sets with varying characteristics, particularly in terms of their wide range of IRs. It is necessary to evaluate the performance of the proposed QOSM on a larger number of high-quality data sets, considering different IRs. In addition, in the future work, we plan to separately assess the performance of QOSM on data sets with imbalance and overlapping, to study the combined and individual influence of these two factors on classification performance.</p>
<p>While the proposed QOSM can be seamlessly integrated with various classifiers, we chose not to include deep learning-based classification methods in our analysis due to their usual requirement for large data sets for effective training, which was not met by the data set used in this study. We acknowledge that if we were to acquire a larger data set characterized by high imbalance and overlap, we would be eager to investigate the performance of deep learning-based methods in conjunction with QOSM.</p></section></section></section><section id="section20-15353702231220665"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">Conclusions</h2>
<p>In this study, we introduced QOSM, a novel oversampling method based on quantum potential theory, to address the challenges posed by highly imbalanced and overlapped data sets in classification tasks. By leveraging the constructive covering algorithm and quantum potential theory, QOSM selects cover centers based on their potential energy, resulting in improved representation of the original sample distribution, particularly in the overlapped region. In addition, QOSM calculates the centroid of minority covers with their KNNs, allowing for the generation of synthetic samples through linear interpolation. Compared to SMOTE and its variants, QOSM considers both the individual sample relationships and the relationships between covers, which mitigates the impact of noisy data and reduces the generation of mislabeled synthetic samples in highly imbalanced data sets.</p>
<p>Our experiments involved three traditional classifiers (SVM, KNN, and NB) and 10 public data sets characterized by high IRs and significant overlap. The results demonstrate that QOSM significantly enhances classification accuracy when compared to approaches that do not address the imbalance and overlap in the data. Furthermore, QOSM consistently outperforms three existing oversampling methods when evaluated with the same classifiers and data sets. Due to its classifier independence, QOSM can be effectively combined with various classifiers, offering promising potential for improving classification accuracy on highly imbalanced and overlapped data.</p>
<p>In conclusion, QOSM introduces a novel approach to tackle the challenges of imbalance and overlap in classification tasks. Through comprehensive evaluations and comparisons, we have shown its effectiveness in improving classification accuracy and its superiority over existing oversampling methods. With its versatility and potential for integration with different classifiers, QOSM provides a valuable tool for addressing imbalanced and overlapped data in real-world scenarios.</p></section><section id="fn-group1" class="fn-group"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">Footnotes</h2>
